He has now a claim on the injurer; he gets him into his power, is able to confer benefits on him and force upon him all that he wishes. The unhappy injurer, thus punished by the reception of wealth, finds himself helpless; and where the injury is great, the injured man may bestow upon the other all his wealth and attain to the envied condition of a pauper.

Among the Kosekin the sick are objects of the highest regard. All classes vie with one another in their attentions. The rich send their luxuries; the paupers, however, not having anything to give, go themselves and wait on them and nurse them. For this there is no help, and the rich grumble, but can do nothing. The sick are thus sought out incessantly, and most carefully tended. When they die there is great rejoicing, since death is a blessing; but the nurses labor hard to preserve them in life, so as to prolong the enjoyment of the high privilege of nursing. Of all sick the incurable are most honored, since they require nursing always. Children also are highly honored and esteemed, and the aged too, since both classes require the care of others and must be the recipients of favors which all are anxious to bestow. Those who suffer from contagious diseases are more sought after than any other class, for in waiting on these there is the chance of gaining the blessing of death; indeed, in these cases much trouble is usually experienced from the rush of those who insist on offering their services.

===Chinese===
从没有人故意加害于人，加害只出于无意；而这种事一旦发生，受害的一方反倒欢喜无量。他从此便对加害者有了主权：把对方攥在手心里，可以向对方施恩，把自己愿意给的一切硬塞给对方。那倒霉的加害者就这样因被迫领受财富而受罚，束手无策；倘使伤害重大，受害方便可把自己的全部家财都赠与对方，从而登上了人人艳羡的赤贫者之境。

在科塞金人中间，病人是最受尊崇的对象。各阶级争相殷勤看护。富人们送去自己的珍馐美物；赤贫者们却因无物可赠，便亲身前去伺候、照料。这实在无从阻挡，富人们怨声连连，却也无可奈何。病人就这样被人寻访不辍，照料得无微不至。他们一旦死去，便有盛大的欢庆，因为死是福分；但看护者们仍苦苦设法维持他们的性命，好延长自己享受看护这一殊恩的时日。在一切病人中，不治之症者最受尊礼，因为他们需要经年累月的看护。孩童也备受尊礼敬重，老人亦然，因为这两类人都须旁人照料，注定要领受众人都急于施与的恩惠。患时疫之人比任何阶级都更遭争抢，因为伺候他们便有赢得死亡之福的机会；说实话，每逢这种病例，争着前来效劳的人蜂拥而至，往往惹出许多麻烦。
===Original===
For it must never be forgotten that the Kosekin love death as we love life; and this accounts for all those ceremonies which to me were so abhorrent, especially the scenes of the Mista Kosek. To them a dead human body is no more than the dead body of a bird: there is no awe felt, no sense of sanctity, of superstitious horror; and so I learned, with a shudder, that the hate of life is a far worse thing than the fear of death. This desire for death is, then, a master-passion, and is the key to all their words and acts. They rejoice over the death of friends, since those friends have gained the greatest of blessings; they rejoice also at the birth of children, since those who are born will one day gain the bliss of death.

For a couple to fall in love is the signal for mutual self-surrender. Each insists on giving up the loved one; and the more passionate the love is, the more eager is the desire to have the loved one married to someone else. Lovers have died brokenhearted from being compelled to marry one another. Poets here among the Kosekin celebrate unhappy love which has met with this end. These poets also celebrate defeats instead of victories, since it is considered glorious for one nation to sacrifice itself to another; but to this there are important limitations, as we shall see. Poets also celebrate street-sweepers, scavengers, lamplighters, laborers, and above all, paupers, and pass by as unworthy of notice the authors, Meleks, and Kohens of the land.

===Chinese===
因为千万不可忘记：科塞金人之爱死，一如我们之爱生；我在他们那些典礼中所感到的种种憎怖，尤其是米斯塔·科塞克的种种场面，缘由全在于此。在他们看来，一具人的尸体同一只鸟的尸体并无分别：既不敬畏，也没有什么神圣之感、迷信之惧；于是我打着寒噤懂得了：厌生远比怕死更可怕。这种求死之欲是一种主宰一切的激情，是他们一切言行的钥匙。朋友死了，他们欢庆，因为死者已获得至大的福分；婴儿降生，他们也欢庆，因为降生者终有一天将得到死亡之福。

一对男女堕入情网，便是互相舍身的信号。双方都执意要把所爱的人让出去；爱得越炽烈，便越急切地盼着所爱者另嫁旁人、另娶旁人。曾有情侣因被逼着彼此成婚而心碎而死。在科塞金人这里，诗人们歌咏的正是这种落得如此下场的苦恋。这些诗人还歌咏败绩而不歌咏胜利，因为一族为异族牺牲自己，被视为无上光荣；不过这里面还有重要的例外，下文便见分晓。诗人们还歌咏扫街人、清道夫、点灯人与劳作者，而尤其歌咏赤贫者；至于本国的文人们、美勒克们与科恩们，则被弃如敝屣，不值一顾。
===Original===
The paupers here form the most honorable class. Next to these are the laborers. These have strikes as with us; but it is always for harder work, longer hours, or smaller pay. The contest between capital and labor rages, but the conditions are reversed; for the grumbling capitalist complains that the laborer will not take as much pay as he ought to while the laborer thinks the capitalist too persistent in his efforts to force money upon him.

Here among the Kosekin the wealthy class forms the mass of the people, while the aristocratic few consist of the paupers. These are greatly envied by the others, and have many advantages. The cares and burdens of wealth, as well as wealth itself, are here considered a curse, and from all these the paupers are exempt. There is a perpetual effort on the part of the wealthy to induce the paupers to accept gifts, just as among us the poor try to rob the rich. Among the wealthy there is a great and incessant murmur at the obstinacy of the paupers. Secret movements are sometimes set on foot which aim at a redistribution of property and a levelling of all classes, so as to reduce the haughty paupers to the same condition as the mass of the nation. More than once there has been a violent attempt at a revolution, so as to force wealth on the paupers; but as a general thing these movements have been put down and their leaders severely punished. The paupers have shown no mercy in their hour of triumph; they have not conceded one jot to the public demand, and the unhappy conspirators have been condemned to increased wealth and luxury, while the leaders have been made Meleks and Kohens. Thus there are among the Kosekin the unfortunate many who are cursed with wealth, and the fortunate few who are blessed with poverty. These walk while the others ride, and from their squalid huts look proudly and contemptuously upon the palaces of their unfortunate fellow-countrymen.

===Chinese===
赤贫者在这里构成最尊贵的阶级。其次是劳作者。他们也有罢工，一如我们那里；但争的从来是更重的活计、更长的工时，或更少的工钱。劳资之争同样如火如荼，只是情势全然倒转：怨声连连的资方抱怨劳工不肯领取应得的足额工钱，而劳工则嫌资方硬把钱塞给自己，未免太纠缠不休。

在科塞金人这里，富人阶级构成了民众的大多数，而贵族少数却是赤贫者。他们深受其余人等的艳羡，占尽种种便宜。财富的烦累与重负，连同财富本身，在这里都被视为诅咒，而赤贫者于这一切悉数得免。富人们无时不极力诱使赤贫者收下馈赠，恰如在我们那里穷人总想劫掠富人。富人们之间对赤贫者的顽固有一场经久不息的怨声。有时还会兴起秘密运动，旨在重分产业、拉平各阶级，好叫高傲的赤贫者沦落到与国中大众相同的境地。用暴力闹一场革命、把财富硬加给赤贫者的企图，也不止一次有过；但大体说来，这类运动总被镇压下去，为首者严惩不贷。赤贫者们在得胜之时从未发过半点慈悲：对公众的要求寸步不让，那些倒霉的密谋者被判加增财富与奢华，为首的则被授予美勒克与科恩之位。于是科塞金人当中，便有了受财富诅咒的不幸多数，与蒙贫穷赐福的幸运少数。这少数人徒步而行，任其余的人骑行乘坐，从自己鄙陋的茅屋里，傲然而轻蔑地睥睨着不幸的同胞们的华堂大厦。
===Original===
The love of death leads to perpetual efforts on the part of each to lay down his life for another. This is a grave difficulty in hunts and battles. Confined prisoners dare not fly, for in such an event the guards kill themselves. This leads to fresh rigors in the captivity of the prisoners in case of their recapture, for they are overwhelmed with fresh luxuries and increased splendors. Finally, if a prisoner persists and is recaptured, he is solemnly put to death, not, as with us, by way of severity, but as the last and greatest honor. Here extremes meet; and death, whether for honor or dishonor, is all the same﻿—death﻿—and is reserved for desperate cases. But among the Kosekin this lofty destiny is somewhat embittered by the agonizing thought on the part of the prisoner, who thus gains it, that his wretched family must be doomed, not, as with us, to poverty and want, but, on the contrary, to boundless wealth and splendor.

Among so strange a people it seemed singular to me what offences could possibly be committed which could be regarded and punished as crimes. These, however, I soon found out. Instead of robbers, the Kosekin punished the secret bestowers of their wealth on others. This is regarded as a very grave offence. Analogous to our crime of piracy is the forcible arrest of ships at sea and the transfer to them of valuables. Sometimes the Kosekin pirates give themselves up as slaves. Kidnapping, assault, highway robbery, and crimes of violence have their parallel here in cases where a strong man, meeting a weaker, forces himself upon him as his slave or compels him to take his purse. If the weaker refuse, the assailant threatens to kill himself, which act would lay the other under obligations to receive punishment from the State in the shape of gifts and honors, or at least subject him to unpleasant inquiries. Murder has its counterpart among the Kosekin in cases where one man meets another, forces money on him, and kills himself. Forgery occurs where one uses another’s name so as to confer money on him.

===Chinese===
尚死之心，使人人无时不想为他人舍出性命。这在围猎和战阵中是一桩极大的难事。被囚的囚犯不敢脱逃，因为一旦逃脱，看守们便会自尽。于是囚犯一旦被追回，囚禁便愈发严酷——他们将被新的珍馐与更盛的奢华所吞没。到末了，倘若囚犯执意再逃、又被擒回，他便被隆重地处死——不像在我们那里出于严惩，而是作为最后的、至大的荣耀。物极必反，两极相通：死亡，无论出于荣宠还是羞辱，横竖是一回事——死——专留给走投无路的案子。但在科塞金人这里，这份崇高的归宿也不免掺着苦味：那赢得此荣的囚犯一想到自家可怜的妻儿亲眷注定要遭殃——不像在我们那里沦为贫匮乏靠，而是恰恰相反，沦为无穷无尽的财富与奢华——便五内如焚。

在如此奇特的族人中间，究竟还能犯下什么罪行、被当作罪来惩办，我起初觉得不可思议。不过这层我很快就弄明白了。科塞金人不惩办劫盗，惩办的是暗中把家财赠与旁人的人。这被视为极其严重的罪过。与我们那里的海盗罪相当的，是在海上强行拦截船只、把财宝搬运过船的行径。有时科塞金的海盗索性把自己送上船去充当奴隶。绑架、殴打、拦路抢劫乃至种种暴行，在这里的对应情形是：强者遇上弱者，硬要充当对方的奴隶，或逼对方收下自己的钱袋。弱者若不肯依从，行凶者便以自尽相胁——这一死便叫对方背上干系，须受国家以馈赠荣宠之形施加的惩罚，至少也要吃一番盘诘、落一场没趣。谋杀在科塞金人这里的对应情形是：一个人遇上另一个，硬把钱塞给对方，随即自尽。伪造文书的情形则是冒用他人名义，好把钱塞到对方名下。
===Original===
There are many other crimes, all of which are severely punished. The worse the offence is, the better is the offender treated. Among the Kosekin capital punishment is imprisonment amid the greatest splendor, where the prisoner is treated like a king, and has many palaces and great retinues; for that which we consider the highest they regard as the lowest, and with them the chief post of honor is what we would call the lowest menial office. Of course, among such a people, any suffering from want is unknown, except when it is voluntary. The pauper class, with all their great privileges, have this restriction, that they are forced to receive enough for food and clothing. Some, indeed, manage by living in out-of-the-way places to deprive themselves of these, and have been known to die of starvation; but this is regarded as dishonorable, as taking an undue advantage of a great position, and where it can be proved, the children and relatives of the offender are severely punished according to the Kosekin fashion.

State politics here move, like individual affairs, upon the great principle of contempt for earthly things. The State is willing to destroy itself for the good of other states; but as other states are in the same position, nothing can result. In times of war the object of each army is to honor the other and benefit it by giving it the glory of defeat. The contest is thus most fierce. The Kosekin, through their passionate love of death, are terrible in battle; and when they are also animated by the desire to confer glory on their enemies by defeating them, they generally succeed in their aim. This makes them almost always victorious, and when they are not so not a soul returns alive. Their state of mind is peculiar. If they are defeated they rejoice, since defeat is their chief glory; but if they are victorious they rejoice still more in the benevolent thought that they have conferred upon the enemy the joy, the glory, and the honor of defeat.

===Chinese===
此外还有许多别的罪，一律严惩不贷。罪行越重，罪犯所得的待遇反而越优。在科塞金人这里，极刑是在无上豪奢中的圈禁：囚犯被奉若君王，拥有许多宫殿和浩大的扈从；因为我们视为最高的，他们偏视为最低，在他们那里，荣宠之极的职位，正是我们所说的最卑贱的贱役。当然，在这样一个民族中间，匮乏之苦是无人知晓的，除非出于自愿。赤贫者阶级虽有种种特权，却受着这样一条约束：他们被迫领受足以衣食的用度。确实有些人设法遁居穷乡僻壤，把这少许也摆脱掉，据知还真有饿死的；但这被视为不名誉之事，是滥用崇高地位的便宜，一旦查实，罪犯的子女亲眷便要依科塞金的规矩严加惩办。

邦国的政务也如私人事务一样，循着鄙薄尘世之物的大原则运行。国家甘愿为了他国的利益自我毁灭；无奈旁的国家也处在同一境地，于是万事都成不了局。战时，每支军队的目标都是尊崇敌军、成全敌军，把败绩的荣光让与对方。争斗因此空前惨烈。科塞金人出于炽烈的尚死之情，在战阵中凶悍无比；当他们再怀着以击败敌人来赠敌以荣耀的愿望时，大抵总能如愿。这使他们几乎战无不胜；而一旦不胜，便无一生还。他们的心境颇为奇特：败了，他们欢庆，因为败绩是他们的至上荣光；胜了，他们愈发欢庆，怀着一片仁心，庆幸自己把败北的欢乐、光荣与荣耀赠给了敌人。
===Original===
Here all shrink from governing others. The highest wish of each is to serve. The Meleks and Kohens, whom I at first considered the highest, are really the lowest orders; next to these come the authors, then the merchants, then farmers, then artisans, then laborers, and, finally, the highest rank is reached in the paupers. Happy the aristocratic, the haughty, the envied paupers! The same thing is seen in their armies. The privates here are highest in rank, and the officers come next in different graduations. These officers, however, have the command and the charge of affairs as with us; yet this is consistent with their position, for here to obey is considered nobler than to command. In the fleet the rowers are the highest class; next come the fighting-men; and lowest of all are the officers. War arises from motives as peculiar as those which give rise to private feuds; as, for instance, where one nation tries to force a province upon another; where they try to make each other greater; where they try to benefit unduly each other’s commerce; where one may have a smaller fleet or army than has been agreed on, or where an ambassador has been presented with gifts, or received too great honor or attention.

In such a country as this, where riches are disliked and despised, I could not imagine how people could be induced to engage in trade. This, however, was soon explained. The laborers and artisans have to perform their daily work, so as to enable the community to live and move and have its being. Their impelling motive is the high one of benefiting others most directly. They refuse anything but the very smallest pay, and insist on giving for this the utmost possible labor. Tradesmen also have to supply the community with articles of all sorts; merchants have to sail their ships to the same end﻿—all being animated by the desire of effecting the good of others. Each one tries not to make money, but to lose it; but as the competition is sharp and universal, this is difficult, and the larger portion are unsuccessful. The purchasers are eager to pay as much as possible, and the merchants and traders grow rich in spite of their utmost endeavors. The wealthy classes go into business so as to lose money, but in this they seldom succeed. It has been calculated that only two percent in every community succeed in reaching the pauper class. The tendency is for all the labors of the working-class to be ultimately turned upon the unfortunate wealthy class. The workmen being the creators of wealth, and refusing to take adequate pay, cause a final accumulation of the wealth of the community in the hands of the mass of the non-producers, who thus are fixed in their unhappy position, and can hope for no escape except by death. The farmers till the ground, the fishermen fish, the laborers toil, and the wealth thus created is pushed from these incessantly till it all falls upon the lowest class﻿—namely, the rich, including Athons, Meleks, and Kohens. It is a burden that is often too heavy to be borne; but there is no help for it, and the better-minded seek to cultivate resignation.

===Chinese===
在这里，人人都避之唯恐不及的是统治他人。每个人的最高愿望是服侍人。美勒克与科恩——起初我把他们当作至贵——其实是最低的等级；其次是文人们，再其次是商人，然后是农户、匠人、劳作者，最后，至高的品级便在赤贫者身上达到。那贵族的、高傲的、人人艳羡的赤贫者们，何其有福！他们的军队里也见出同样的道理。列兵在这里品级最高，军官们依高低等第次之。不过这些军官也同我们那里一样握有指挥、执掌事务之权；而这与其地位并不相悖，因为在这里，服从被视为比指挥更高贵。在舰队里，桨手是最高的一等；其次是战斗人员；至低的则是军官。战争的起因，也同私仇的缘由一样奇特：譬如一国硬要把一行省塞给另一国；譬如两国都想使对方更强大；譬如彼此都想格外有益于对方的商务；譬如一方拥有的舰队或军队比约定数目为少；又譬如一位使臣受了馈赠，或得了过分的尊荣与殷勤。

在这样一个财富遭人嫌恶鄙夷的国度里，我实在想象不出，人们何以还肯从事商业。不过这层很快就有了分晓。劳作者与匠人必须做每日的活计，好教整个群体得以生活、动作、存留。推动他们的动机是崇高的：最直接地造福他人。他们非极少的工钱一概不收，而为了这点工钱，又执意付出最大限度的劳力。店铺商人也必须为群体供应各色物品；商人必须为此扬帆行船——人人怀着的都是成就他人之福的愿望。个个都力求的不是赚钱，而是赔钱；无奈竞争激烈而普遍，这实在难办，大多数人免不了失败。买主们抢着多付钱款，商人店主们尽管竭尽全力，还是日渐富了起来。富人们经商是为了赔本，但他们在这上头也难得如愿。据人测算，每个群体当中，能跻身赤贫者之列的不过百分之二。万事的总趋势是：劳动阶级的一切劳作，最终都落到不幸的富人阶级头上。工人们既是财富的创造者，又拒绝领取相称的报酬，便致使群体的财富最终积聚到大多数不事生产的人手里；这些人就此钉死在自己的不幸地位上，除了死，别无指望脱身。农户耕田，渔人打鱼，劳作者流汗，这样创造出的财富，又被人不停地从他们手里推出去，直到尽数落到最低的阶级——即富人身上，连阿通、美勒克与科恩也一并包括在内。这副担子常常重得扛不动；却又无可奈何，心地较好的人便只得修习顺从之道。
===Original===
Women and men are in every respect absolutely equal, holding precisely the same offices and doing the same work. In general, however, it is observed that women are a little less fond of death than men, and a little less unwilling to receive gifts. For this reason they are very numerous among the wealthy class, and abound in the offices of administration. Women serve in the army and navy as well as men, and from their lack of ambition or energetic perseverance they are usually relegated to the lower ranks, such as officers and generals. To my mind it seemed as though the women were in all the offices of honor and dignity, but in reality it was the very opposite. The same is true in the family. The husbands insist on giving everything to the wives and doing everything for them. The wives are therefore universally the rulers of the household while the husbands have an apparently subordinate, but, to the Kosekin, a more honorable position.

As to the religion of the Kosekin, I could make nothing of it. They believe that after death they go to what they call the world of darkness. The death they long for leads to the darkness that they love; and the death and the darkness are eternal. Still, they persist in saying that the death and the darkness together form a state of bliss. They are eloquent about the happiness that awaits them there in the sunless land﻿—the world of darkness; but for my own part, it always seemed to me a state of nothingness.

===Chinese===
女子与男子在各方面绝对平等，担任完全相同的职司，做同样的活计。不过一般都看得出，女子对死的爱恋比男子稍逊，对收受馈赠也不那么推拒。因此她们在富人阶级中人数极多，在行政各职司中更是比比皆是。女子同男子一样在陆军海军中服役，但由于她们缺乏雄心，也不肯奋发坚忍，通常被贬到较低的品级里去，诸如军官、将官之类。在我眼里，仿佛凡有荣衔尊位的职司尽被女子占着，其实恰恰相反。家庭里也是同样的道理。丈夫们执意把一切都让给妻子，事事都替她们做。于是家家户户无不由妻子当家作主，而丈夫们所处的地位看似低伏，在科塞金人看来却更为尊荣。

至于科塞金人的宗教，我全然参不透。他们相信人死后便去往他们所谓的黑暗之境。他们苦苦求死，为的是通往他们所热爱的黑暗；死与黑暗都是永恒的。然而他们偏说，死与黑暗合在一起，构成一种至福之境。他们滔滔不绝地讲说着在那不见天日之邦——黑暗之境——等待他们的幸福；至于我，总觉得那是一种归于虚无的境界。

## XVII Belief and Unbelief / 十七、信与不信

===Original===
The doctor was here interrupted by Featherstone, who, with a yawn, informed him that it was eleven o’clock, and that human endurance had its limits. Upon this the doctor rolled up the manuscript and put it aside for the night, after which supper was ordered.

“Well,” said Featherstone, “what do you think of this last?”

“It contains some very remarkable statements,” said the doctor.

===Chinese===
医生正读到这里，被费瑟斯通勋爵打断了；后者打了个哈欠，告诉他已经十一点钟，还说人的忍耐终归有限度。医生闻言便把手稿卷起，搁过一夜，随后吩咐开晚饭。

「那么，」费瑟斯通说，「这最后一段，你们作何感想？」

「里头有几条非同寻常的论断。」医生说。

===Original===
“There are certainly monsters enough in it,” said Melick﻿—

> “ ‘Gorgons, and hydras, and chimeras dire.’ ”

“Well, why not?” said the doctor.

“It seems to me,” said Melick, “that the writer of this has peopled his world with creatures that resemble the fossil animals more than anything else.”

===Chinese===
「那里的怪物倒也管够，」梅利克说——

> 「『蛇发妖，九头蛇，还有狰狞的喷火怪。』」

「哦，为什么就不能有？」医生说。

「依我看，」梅利克说，「写这篇东西的人，用来住满他那个世界的生灵，与其说像别的任何东西，不如说最像各种化石动物。」

===Original===
“The so-called fossil animals,” said the doctor, “may not be extinct. There are fossil specimens of animals that still have living representatives. There is no reason why many of those supposed to be extinct may not be alive now. It is well known that many very remarkable animals have become extinct within a comparatively recent period. These great birds, of which More speaks, seem to me to belong to these classes. The dodo was in existence fifty years ago, the moa about a hundred years ago. These great birds, together with others, such as the epiornis and palapteryx, have disappeared, not through the ordinary course of nature, but by the hand of man. Even in our hemisphere they may yet be found. Who can tell but that the moa or the dodo may yet be lurking somewhere here in the interior of Madagascar, of Borneo, or of Papua?”

===Chinese===
「那些所谓的化石动物，」医生说，「未必就灭绝了。有些动物虽有化石标本，如今却仍有活着的同类。没有理由认为，许多被推定灭绝的动物如今不在世间存活。众所周知，若干非常了不起的动物，是在较近的时期内才归于灭绝的。莫尔所说的这些巨鸟，依我看就属于这些类群。渡渡鸟（dodo）五十年前还存在于世，恐鸟（moa）约一百年前还在。这些巨鸟，连同象鸟（epiornis）、帕拉普特里克斯鸟（Palapteryx）之类，其所以绝迹，并非循自然界寻常的轨辙，而是丧于人类之手。即便在我们这个半球，它们也未必没有遗种。谁说得上呢？说不定恐鸟或渡渡鸟，此刻正潜藏在马达加斯加、婆罗洲或巴布亚的内陆某处？」

===Original===
“Can you make out anything about those great birds?” asked Featherstone. “Do they resemble anything that exists now, or has ever existed?”

“Well, yes, I think so,” said the doctor. “Unfortunately, More is not at all close or accurate in his descriptions; he has a decidedly unscientific mind, and so one cannot feel sure; yet from his general statements I think I can decide pretty nearly upon the nature and the scientific name of each one of his birds and animals. It is quite evident to me that most of these animals belong to races that no longer exist among us, and that this world at the South Pole has many characteristics which are like those of what is known as the Coal Period. I allude in particular to the vast forests of fern, of gigantic grasses and reeds. At the same time the general climate and the atmosphere seem like what we may find in the tropics at present. It is evident that in More’s world various epochs are represented, and that animals of different ages are living side by side.”

===Chinese===
「那些巨鸟，你能考究出个所以然来么？」费瑟斯通问。「它们像不像如今尚存、或曾经存在过的什么东西？」

「呃，能，我想能，」医生说。「可惜莫尔的描述毫不精细、也不准确；他的头脑全无科学素养，因此不能遽下断语；不过凭他那些大概的说法，我想我大体能判定他笔下每一种飞禽走兽的性质与学名。在我看来甚为明显：这些动物多半属于在我们这里早已不复存在的族类；而这南极的世界，具有许多与所谓石炭纪（Coal Period）相似的特征。我特别指那漫无际涯的蕨类森林、巨型的禾草与芦苇。与此同时，其总体的气候与大气，又仿佛是我们今日在热带所见。显而易见，在莫尔的那个世界里，各个地质时代杂然并存，不同时代的动物比肩而活。」

===Original===
“What do you think of the *opkuk*?” asked Featherstone, with a yawn.

“Well, I hardly know.”

“Why, it must be a dodo, of course,” said Melick, “only magnified.”

“That,” said the doctor, gravely, “is a thought that naturally suggests itself; but then the *opkuk* is certainly far larger than the dodo.”

“Oh, More put on his magnifying-glasses just then.”

“The dodo,” continued the doctor, taking no notice of this, “in other respects corresponds with More’s description of the *opkuk*. Clusius and Bontius give good descriptions and there is a well-known picture of one in the British Museum. It is a massive, clumsy bird, ungraceful in its form with heavy movements, wings too short for flight, little or no tail, and down rather than feathers. The body, according to Bontius, is as big as that of the African ostrich, but the legs are very short. It has a large head, great black eyes, long bluish-white bill, ending in a beak like that of a vulture, yellow legs, thick and short, four toes on each foot solid, long, and armed with sharp black claws. The flesh particularly on the breast, is fat and esculent. Now, all this corresponds with More’s account, except as to the size of the two, for the *opkuks* are as large as oxen.”

===Chinese===
「你觉得奥普库克如何？」费瑟斯通打着哈欠问。

「呃，我说不好。」

「嗐，那准是只渡渡鸟嘛，」梅利克说，「不过放大了几号。」

「这个念头，」医生正色道，「自然会自己冒上来；只是奥普库克确实比渡渡鸟大得多。」

「哦，那会儿莫尔准是戴上了他的放大镜。」

「渡渡鸟，」医生不理会这句插话，继续说道，「在其余各方面都与莫尔对奥普库克的描述相合。克卢修斯（Clusius）与邦提乌斯（Bontius）对它有很好的记述，大英博物馆里还藏有一幅尽人皆知的渡渡鸟图。它是一种笨重臃肿的鸟，体态不雅，行动迟滞，双翼短得飞不起来，几乎没有尾巴，周身披羽近于绒毛。据邦提乌斯说，它的躯干有非洲鸵鸟那么大，双腿却极短。头颅硕大，眼睛又黑又大，长喙泛着蓝白色，末端弯成兀鹫般的钩喙；双腿黄色，粗而短，每脚四趾，坚实而长，末端生着锋利的黑爪。它的肉，尤其是胸肉，肥厚可食。凡此种种，都与莫尔的记述吻合，唯独体型一项除外——奥普库克有牛那么大。」

===Original===
“Oh, that’s nothing,” said Melick; “I’m determined to stand up for the dodo.” With this he burst forth singing﻿—

> “Oh, the dodo once lived, but he doesn’t live now;
 

 Yet why should a cloud overshadow our brow?
 

 The loss of that bird ne’er should trouble our brains,
 

 For though he is gone, still our claret remains.
 

 Sing do-do﻿—jolly do-do!
 

 Hurrah! in his name let our cups overflow.”

===Chinese===
「哦，那不算回事，」梅利克说，「我可打定主意要替渡渡鸟出头。」说着他便引吭高歌起来——

> 「哦，渡渡鸟曾活在世上，如今却一命呜呼；
 

 可干吗要教一朵愁云，罩住我们的眉目？
 

 那鸟儿一去，本不该搅扰我们的心神，
 

 它虽已不在，红酒犹自满樽。
 

 唱多——都，快活的多——都！
 

 好哇！以它的名义，让我们的酒杯满得四溢！」

===Original===
“As for your definition, doctor,” continued Melick, “I’ll give you one worth a dozen of yours:

> “ ’Twas a mighty bird; those strong, short legs were never known to fail,
 

 And he felt a glory of pride while thinking of that little tail,
 

 And his beak was marked with vigor, curving like a wondrous hook;
 

 Thick and ugly was his body﻿—such a form as made one look!”

===Chinese===
「至于你那段描述，医生，」梅利克接着说，「我回敬你一段，一个顶你一打：

> 「那是一只雄壮的大鸟；那双有力的短腿，向来稳当，从不打晃，
 

 它一想起自己那条小尾巴，胸中便涌起一片荣光，
 

 它的喙威风凛凛，弯成一钩，奇妙无双，
 

 它的身子又粗又丑——那副模样，偏教人看了还想多望！」

===Original===
“Melick,” said Featherstone, “you’re a volatile youth. You mustn’t mind him, doctor. He’s a professional cynic, sceptic, and scoffer. Oxenden and I, however, are open to conviction, and want to know more about those birds and beasts. Can you make anything out of the *opmahera*?”

The doctor swallowed a glass of wine, and replied:

“Oh yes; there are many birds, each of which may be the *opmahera*. There’s the fossil bird of Massachusetts, of which nothing is left but the footprints; but some of these are eighteen inches in length, and show a stride of two yards. The bird belonged to the order of the Grallae, and may have been ten or twelve feet in height. Then there is the *Gastornis parisiensis*, which was as tall as an ostrich, as big as an ox, and belongs to the same order as the other. Then there is the Palapteryx, of which remains have been found in New Zealand, which was seven or eight feet in height. But the one which to my mind is the real counterpart of the *opmahera* is the *Dinornis gigantea*, whose remains are also found in New Zealand. It is the largest bird known, with long legs, a long neck, and short wings, useless for flight. One specimen that has been found is upward of thirteen feet in height. There is no reason why some should not have been much taller. More compares its height to that of a giraffe. The Maoris call this bird the *Moa*, and their legends and traditions are full of mention of it. When they first came to the island, six or seven hundred years ago, they found these vast birds everywhere, and hunted them for food. To my mind the dinornis is the *opmahera* of More. As to riding on them, that is likely enough; for ostriches are used for this purpose, and the dinornis must have been far stronger and fleeter than the ostrich. It is possible that some of these birds may still be living in the remoter parts of our hemisphere.”

===Chinese===
「梅利克，」费瑟斯通说，「你这个年轻人，心性太浮。别理会他，医生。他是个职业性的愤世者、怀疑派、讥诮家。我和奥克森登倒是虚心待教，还想多知道些那些飞禽走兽的来历。那奥普玛赫拉，你能考究出什么来么？」

医生吞下一杯酒，答道：

「哦，能；世上鸟类甚多，哪一种都可能是奥普玛赫拉。有马萨诸塞的化石巨鸟，遗存至今的只有脚印，可有些脚印长达十八英寸，步幅足有两码；那鸟属于涉禽类（Grallae），身高或许有十到十二英尺。又有巴黎加斯顿鸟（Gastornis parisiensis），高如鸵鸟，大如牛，与前一种同属一目。又有帕拉普特里克斯鸟，遗骸发现于新西兰，高七八英尺。但依我看，真正与奥普玛赫拉相当的，是巨大恐鸟（Dinornis gigantea），遗骸同样发现于新西兰。它是已知最大的鸟：长腿，长颈，短翼，派不上飞翔的用场。已发现的一件标本，身高逾十三英尺，没有理由认为不会再有高得多的个体。莫尔把它的身高比作长颈鹿。毛利人称这鸟为恐鸟（Moa），他们的传说与歌谣里满是它的名字。他们六七百年前初到岛上时，遍地都是这种巨鸟，还捕以为食。依我之见，恐鸟（dinornis）就是莫尔的奥普玛赫拉。至于骑乘它们，大有可能；鸵鸟尚且供人骑乘，恐鸟必定远比鸵鸟强壮、善跑。这些鸟类之中，说不定有些至今仍存活在我们这个半球更僻远的地方。」

===Original===
“What about those monsters,” asked Featherstone, “that More speaks of in the sacred hunt?”

“I think,” said the doctor, “that I understand pretty well what they were, and can identify them all. As the galley passed the estuary of that great river, you remember that he mentions seeing them on the shore. One may have been the Ichthyosaurus. This, as the name implies, is a fish-lizard. It has the head of a lizard, the snout of a dolphin, the teeth of an alligator, enormous eyes, whose membrane is strengthened by a bony frame, the vertebræ of fishes, sternum and shoulder-bones like those of the lizard, and the fins of a whale. Bayle calls it the whale of the saurians. Another may have been the Cheirotherium. On account of the hand-shaped marks made by its paws, Owen thinks that it was akin to the frogs; but it was a formidable monster, with head and jaws of a crocodile. Another may have been the Teleosaurus, which resembled our alligators. It was thirty-five feet in length. Then there was the Hylaeosaurus, a monster twenty-five feet in length, with a cuirass of bony plates.”

===Chinese===
「那么神圣狩猎中莫尔说起的那些巨怪呢？」费瑟斯通问。

「我想，」医生说，「它们是什么，我心里已了然，且能一一指认。桨帆船驶过那条大河的河口时，你们记得，他提到在岸上望见它们。其一种可能是鱼龙（Ichthyosaurus）。顾名思义，这是鱼蜥之属。它生着蜥蜴的头颅、海豚的吻部、鳄鱼的牙齿，眼睛奇大，护膜由骨质的框格加固；脊椎如鱼，胸骨与肩骨似蜥蜴，鳍似鲸。贝尔（Bayle）称之为蜥蜴族中的鲸。其一种可能是手兽（Cheirotherium），因其足掌留下形似人手的印痕，欧文认为它近于蛙类；但它也是个可畏的巨怪，头颅与颚部一如鳄鱼。其一种又可能是特莱奥蜥（Teleosaurus），形似我们的短吻鳄，身长三十五英尺。还有林龙（Hylaeosaurus），长二十五英尺的巨怪，身披骨板，宛如一领胸铠。」

===Original===
“But none of these correspond with More’s description of the monster that fought with the galley.”

“No,” said the doctor, “I am coming to that now. That monster could have been no other than the Plesiosaurus, one of the most wonderful animals that has ever existed. Imagine a thing with the head of a lizard, the teeth of a crocodile, the neck of a swan, the trunk and tail of a quadruped, and the fins of a whale. Imagine a whale with its head and neck consisting of a serpent, with the strength of the former and the malignant fury of the latter, and then you will have the Plesiosaurus. It was an aquatic animal, yet it had to remain near or on the surface of the water, while its long, serpent-like neck enabled it to reach its prey above or below with swift, far-reaching darts. Yet it had no armor, and could not have been at all a match for the Ichthyosaurus. More’s account shows, however, that it was a fearful enemy for man to encounter.”

“He seems to have been less formidable than that beast which they encountered in the swamp. Have you any idea what that was?”

“I think it can have been no other than the Iguanodon,” said the doctor. “The remains of this animal show that it must have been the most gigantic of all primeval saurians. Judging from existing remains its length was not less than sixty feet, and larger ones may have existed. It stood high on its legs; the hind ones were larger than the fore. The feet were massive and armed with tremendous claws. It lived on the land and fed on herbage. It had a horny, spiky ridge all along its back. Its tail was nearly as long as its body. Its head was short, its jaws enormous, furnished with teeth of a very elaborate structure, and on its muzzle it carried a curved horn. Such a beast as this might well have caused all that destruction of life on the part of his desperate assailants of which More speaks.

===Chinese===
「可是这些没有一个对得上莫尔描写的、同桨帆船搏斗的那头巨怪。」

「不错，」医生说，「我正要说到它。那巨怪不是别的，只能是蛇颈龙（Plesiosaurus），世间曾有过的最奇妙的动物之一。试想一种东西：头似蜥蜴，齿如鳄鱼，颈若天鹅，躯干与尾如四足兽，鳍似鲸。再想象一头鲸，头颈却化作一条巨蛇，兼具前者的膂力与后者的凶暴狂怒，这便是蛇颈龙了。它是水栖动物，却必须留在水面或近水面处；那条长蛇似的颈，使它能上下疾袭、远探如镖，攫取水中水上的猎物。然而它没有铠甲，全然不是鱼龙的对手。不过莫尔的记述表明，人若遇上它，也是个可怕的劲敌。」

「它看去似乎还不及他们在沼泽里遇上的那头巨兽可怕。你知道那是什么么？」

「我想那除了禽龙（Iguanodon），不可能是别的。」医生说。「这动物的遗骸表明，它必是太古蜥蜴类中最巨大的。就现存遗骸判断，其身长不下六十英尺，更大的个体或亦曾有。它双腿直立，身姿高峻；后肢比前肢长大。足掌厚重，爪趾极其犀利。它栖于陆地，以草本为食。背脊从头到尾生着一道角质的棘脊。尾几乎与身等长。头颅短促，颚部极大，牙齿构造极精巧，吻上还生着一只弯角。这样一头巨兽，难怪能叫它那班舍命相搏的攻击者死伤枕藉，正如莫尔所述。」

===Original===
“Then there was another animal,” continued the doctor, who was evidently discoursing upon a favorite topic. “It was the one that came suddenly upon More while he was resting with Almah after his flight with the runaway bird. That I take to be the Megalosaurus. This animal was a monster of tremendous size and strength. Cuvier thought that it might have been seventy feet in length. It was carnivorous, and therefore more ferocious than the Iguanodon, and more ready to attack. Its head was like that of a crocodile, its body massive like that of an elephant, yet larger; its tail was small, and it stood high on its legs, so that it could run with great speed. It was not covered with bony armor, but had probably a hide thick enough to serve the purpose of shell or bone. Its teeth were constructed so as to cut with their edges, and the movement of the jaws produced the combined effect of knife and saw, while their inward curve rendered impossible the escape of prey that had once been caught. It probably frequented the river banks, where it fed upon reptiles of smaller size which inhabited the same places.

“More,” continued the doctor, “is too general in his descriptions. He has not a scientific mind, and he gives but few data; yet I can bring before myself very easily all the scenes which he describes, particularly that one in which the Megalosaurus approaches, and he rushes to mount the dinoris so as to escape. I see that river, with its trees and shrubs, all unknown now except in museums﻿—the vegetation of the Coal Period﻿—the Lepidodendron, the Lepidostrobus, the Pecopteris, the Neuropteris, the Lonchopteris, the Odontopteris, the Sphenopteris, the Cyclopteris, the *Sigellaria veniformis*, the Sphenophyllium, the calamites﻿—”

Melick started to his feet.

“There, there!” he cried, “hold hard, doctor. Talking of calamities, what greater calamity can there be than such a torrent of unknown words? Talk English, doctor, and we shall be able to appreciate you; but to make your jokes, your conundrums, and your brilliant witticisms in a foreign language isn’t fair to us, and does no credit either to your head or your heart.”

The doctor elevated his eyebrows, and took no notice of Melick’s ill-timed levity.

===Chinese===
「还有一种动物，」医生接着说，显然正谈兴大发——这是他心爱的题目。「就是莫尔乘那脱缰的巨鸟逃走之后、偕阿尔玛歇息时突然向他袭来的那头。我断定那是巨齿龙（Megalosaurus）。这动物是个庞然大物，力大无穷。居维叶认为它身长可能达七十英尺。它食肉，因此比禽龙更凶残，也更好攻击。头似鳄鱼，躯体笨重如象而犹过之；尾巴细小，双腿直立，故能疾驰。它不披骨质甲胄，但皮糙肉厚，大概足以充作壳甲之用。它的牙齿构造得宜于以刃切割，双颚的开阖兼收刀锯之效，而牙齿内弯，猎物一经咬住，便再无脱逃之望。它大概常出没于河岸一带，捕食栖居同处的较小爬虫。」

「莫尔，」医生继续说，「描述得太笼统了。他没有科学的头脑，给出的数据寥寥；然而他描绘的种种景象，我却能毫不费力地一一在眼前复活，尤其是巨齿龙逼来、他抢着跨上恐鸟逃走的那一幕。我看见那条河，河边的树木灌丛，如今除博物馆外已无处可见——石炭纪的植被——鳞木（Lepidodendron）、鳞孢穗（Lepidostrobus）、栉羊齿（Pecopteris）、脉羊齿（Neuropteris）、矛羊齿（Lonchopteris）、齿羊齿（Odontopteris）、楔羊齿（Sphenopteris）、圆羊齿（Cyclopteris）、封印木（Sigellaria veniformis）、楔叶（Sphenophyllium）、芦木（calamites）——」

梅利克霍地跳起身来。

「得啦，得啦！」他嚷道，「慢着点儿，医生。才说芦木（calamites），就说上灾殃（calamities）了——天底下还有什么灾殃，大得过这一串不知所云的字眼汇成的洪流？说英国话吧，医生，我们才领略得了你的高论；拿外国语来说笑、打哑谜、卖弄妙语连珠，这对我们不公平，对你的头脑和心肠也都没有光彩。」

医生扬了扬眉毛，对梅利克这不合时宜的轻佻不予理会。

===Original===
“All these stories of strange animals,” said Oxenden, “may be very interesting, doctor, but I must say that I am far more struck by the account of the people themselves. I wonder whether they are an aboriginal race, or descendants of the same stock from which we came?”

“I should say,” remarked the doctor, confidently, “that they are, beyond a doubt, an aboriginal and autochthonous race.”

“I differ from you altogether,” said Oxenden, calmly.

“Oh,” said the doctor, “there can be no doubt about it. Their complexion, small stature, and peculiar eyes﻿—their love of darkness, their singular characteristics, both physical and moral, all go to show that they can have no connection with the races in our part of the Earth.”

===Chinese===
「这些奇禽异兽的故事，」奥克森登说，「也许很有趣，医生，但我得说，更教我惊奇的，还是关于那族人本身的记述。我纳闷，他们究竟是个土生的原住种族，还是同我们出自一脉的后裔？」

「要我说，」医生胸有成竹地说，「他们毫无疑问是个土生土长、原住本地的种族。」

「我同你的看法全然相左。」奥克森登平静地说。

「哦，」医生说，「这还有什么可怀疑的。他们的肤色、矮小的身量、特殊的眼睛——他们的崇暗之癖，他们身心两方面的种种特异之性，全都表明他们同我们这半球的诸种族不可能有任何渊源。」

===Original===
“Their peculiar eyes,” said Oxenden, “are no doubt produced by dwelling in caves for many generations.”

“On the contrary,” said the doctor, “it is their peculiarity of eye that makes them dwell in caves.”

“You are mistaking the cause for the effect, doctor.”

“Not at all; it is you who are making that mistake.”

===Chinese===
「他们那双特殊的眼睛，」奥克森登说，「无疑是世代穴居造成的。」

「恰恰相反，」医生说，「正因眼睛特异，他们才穴居洞中。」

「你把因果弄颠倒了，医生。」

「一点也没有；倒是你弄颠倒了。」

===Original===
“It’s the old debate,” said Melick. “As the poet has it:

> “ ‘Which was first, the egg or the hen?
 

 Tell me, I pray, ye learned men!’ ”

“There are the eyeless fishes of the great cave of Kentucky,” said Oxenden, “whose eyes have become extinct from living in the dark.”

“No,” cried the doctor; “the fish that have arisen in that lake have never needed eyes, and have never had them.”

Oxenden laughed.

===Chinese===
「这又是场老官司了，」梅利克说。「正像诗人唱的：

> 「『先有蛋呢，还是先有鸡？
 

 列位鸿儒，请来解惑释疑！』」

「肯塔基大洞里不是有无眼的鱼么，」奥克森登说，「它们的眼睛就是因为世代居于黑暗，才退化绝迹的。」

「不对，」医生大声说。「生在那湖里的鱼，从来就用不着眼睛，也从来不曾有过眼睛。」

奥克森登笑了。

===Original===
“Well,” said he, “I’ll discuss the question with you on different grounds altogether, and I will show clearly that these men, these bearded men, must belong to a stock that is nearly related to our own, or, at least, that they belong to a race of men with whom we are all very familiar.”

“I should like very much to have you try it,” said the doctor.

“Very well,” said Oxenden. “In the first place, I take their language.”

“Their language!”

===Chinese===
「好吧，」他说，「我要换个全然不同的路数来同你论这个问题。我会明白昭示：这些人，这些蓄着须的人，必属于与我们血统至近的一族；退一步说，至少也属于我们大家极熟悉的一支人类。」

「我倒很乐意看你试一试。」医生说。

「很好，」奥克森登说。「首先，我请出他们的语言来作证。」

「他们的语言！」

===Original===
“Yes. More has given us very many words in their language. Now he himself says that these words had an Arabic sound. He was slightly acquainted with that language. What will you say if I tell you that these words are still more like Hebrew?”

“Hebrew!” exclaimed the doctor, in amazement.

“Yes, Hebrew,” said Oxenden. “They are all very much like Hebrew words, and the difference is not greater than that which exists between the words of any two languages of the Aryan family.”

“Oh, if you come to philology I’ll throw up the sponge,” said the doctor. “Yet I should like to hear what you have to say on that point.”

===Chinese===
「正是。莫尔记下了他们语言中的许多词。他自己说过，这些词带着阿拉伯语的音调。他对那种语言略通一二。可若我告诉你，这些词更像希伯来语，你作何话说？」

「希伯来语！」医生惊呼，讶异万分。

「正是，希伯来语。」奥克森登说。「它们全都酷肖希伯来语的词，其间的差异，并不比雅利安语系任何两种语言的词语之间的差异更大。」

「哦，你若要搬出语言学来，我就缴械认输了，」医生说。「不过你在这点上的高见，我倒愿意领教。」

===Original===
“The languages of the Aryan family,” said Oxenden, “have the same general characteristics, and in all of them the differences that exist in their most common words are subject to the action of a regular law. The action of the law is best seen in the changes which take place in the mutes. These changes are indicated in a summary and comprehensive way by means of what is called ‘Grimm’s Law.’ Take Latin and English, for instance. ‘Grimm’s Law’ tells us, among other things, that in Latin and in that part of English which is of Teutonic origin, a large number of words are essentially the same, and differ merely in certain phonetic changes. Take the word ‘father.’ In Latin, as also in Greek, it is ‘*pater*.’ Now the Latin *p* in English becomes *f*; that is, the thin mute becomes the aspirated mute. The same change may be seen in the Latin ‘*piscis*,’ which in English is ‘fish,’ and the Greek ‘*πυρ*’ which in English is ‘fire.’ Again, if the Latin or Greek word begins with an aspirate, the English word begins with a medial; thus the Latin *f* is found responsive to the English *b*, as in Latin ‘*fagus*,’ English ‘beech,’ Latin ‘*fero*,’ English ‘bear.’ Again, if the Latin or Greek has the medial, the English has the thin, as in Latin ‘*duo*,’ English ‘two,’ Latin ‘*genu*,’ English ‘knee.’ Now, I find that in many of the words which More mentions this same ‘Grimm’s Law’ will apply; and I am inclined to think that if they were spelled with perfect accuracy they would show the same relation between the Kosekin language and the Hebrew that there is between the Saxon English and the Latin.”

The doctor gave a heavy sigh.

===Chinese===
「雅利安语系的诸语言，」奥克森登说，「具有相同的总体特征；在它们最常用的词里，种种差异都受着一条规律整齐的支配。这条规律的作用，在塞音的种种变化里看得最清楚。这些变化，可以借所谓『格林律』（Grimm's Law）作一总括而全面的表述。且拿拉丁语同英语作比。『格林律』告诉我们，除其他事项外：拉丁语与英语中源自条顿语支的那一部分，有大量的词本质相同，只是经历了某些语音变化。拿『父亲』一词来说，拉丁语作 pater，希腊语亦然；拉丁语的 p 到英语里变成 f，也就是说，清塞音变成了送气塞音。同样的变化见于拉丁语 piscis，英语作 fish；希腊语 πυρ，英语作 fire。再者，拉丁或希腊词以送气音开头，英语词便以浊塞音开头；于是拉丁语的 f 与英语的 b 相对应，如拉丁 fagus、英语 beech，拉丁 fero、英语 bear。又，拉丁或希腊语用浊塞音之处，英语便用清塞音，如拉丁 duo、英语 two，拉丁 genu、英语 knee。如今我发现，莫尔提到的许多词里，这条『格林律』同样适用；我倾向于认为，倘使这些词的拼写精确无误，它们便会显示出科塞金语与希伯来语之间的关系，恰如撒克逊英语与拉丁语之间的关系。」

医生沉沉叹了口气。

===Original===
“You’re out of my depth, Oxenden,” said he. “I’m nothing of a philologist.”

“By Jove!” said Featherstone, “I like this. This is equal to your list of the plants of the Coal Period, doctor. But I say, Oxenden, while you are about it, why don’t you give us a little dose of Anglo-Saxon and Sanskrit? By Jove! the fellow has Bopp by heart, and yet he expects us to argue with him.”

“I have it!” cried Melick. “The Kosekin are the lost Ten Tribes. Oxenden is feeling his way to that. He is going to make them out to be all Hebrew; and then, of course, the only conclusion will be that they are the Ten Tribes, who after a life of strange vicissitudes have pulled up at the South Pole. It’s a wonder More didn’t think of that﻿—or the writer of this yarn, whoever he may be. Well, for my part, I always took a deep interest in the lost Ten Tribes, and thought them a fine body of men.”

“Don’t think they’ve got much of the Jew about them,” said Featherstone, languidly. “They hate riches and all that, you know. Break a Jew’s heart to hear of all that property wasted, and money going a-begging. Not a bad idea, though, that of theirs about money. Too much money’s a howwid baw, by Jove!”

===Chinese===
「这可超出我的学力了，奥克森登，」他说。「我全然算不得语言学家。」

「天哪！」费瑟斯通说，「这个我喜欢。这抵得上你那份石炭纪植物名录了，医生。不过我说，奥克森登，索性趁热打铁，怎么不再给我们来上一小剂盎格鲁-撒克逊语和梵语？天哪！这家伙把博普（Bopp）都背得滚瓜烂熟，居然还指望我们同他辩论。」

「我明白了！」梅利克嚷道。「科塞金人就是失落的十支派。奥克森登正朝这上头摸呢。他要证明他们全是希伯来人；那么，顺理成章，唯一的结论便是：他们就是那十支派，历经种种离奇的流离颠沛，最终在南极落了脚。怪的是莫尔竟没想到这一层——或者说，这篇故事的作者，管他是谁呢。呃，就我而言，我向来对失落的十支派关怀深切，觉得那是一群体面人物。」

「我看他们身上没多少犹太味儿，」费瑟斯通懒洋洋地说。「你知道，他们憎恶财富那一套。听闻那么多财产白白糟蹋、钱财无人肯要，犹太人心都要碎的。不过话又说回来，他们那套关于钱财的见解倒不坏。钱太多了可是桩吓——死人的腻烦事，天哪！」

===Original===
“Well,” continued Oxenden, calmly resuming, and taking no notice of these interruptions, “I can give you word after word that More has mentioned which corresponds to a kindred Hebrew word in accordance with ‘Grimm’s Law.’ For instance, Kosekin ‘*Op*,’ Hebrew ‘*Oph*;’ Kosekin ‘*Athon*,’ Hebrew ‘*Adon*;’ Kosekin ‘*Salon*,’ Hebrew ‘*Shalom*.’ They are more like Hebrew than Arabic, just as Anglo-Saxon words are more like Latin or Greek than Sanskrit.”

“Hurrah!” cried Melick, “we’ve got him to Sanskrit at last! Now, Oxenden, my boy, trot out the *Hitopadesa*, the *Megha Dhuta*, the *Rig Veda*. Quote *Beowulf* and Caedmon. Gives us a little Zeno, and wind up with *Lalla Rookh* in modern Persian.”

“So I conclude,” said Oxenden, calmly, ignoring Melick, “that the Kosekin are a Semitic people. Their complexion and their beards show them to be akin to the Caucasian race, and their language proves beyond the shadow of a doubt that they belong to the Semitic branch of that race. It is impossible for an autochthonous people to have such a language.”

===Chinese===
「好吧，」奥克森登从容地接着说，对这些插话不以为意，「莫尔提到的词，我可以一个接一个举给你们看，它们都按照『格林律』与希伯来语的同源词相对应。譬如：科塞金语 Op，希伯来语 Oph；科塞金语 Athon，希伯来语 Adon；科塞金语 Salon，希伯来语 Shalom。它们之更近于希伯来语而非阿拉伯语，恰如盎格鲁-撒克逊语的词之更近于拉丁语或希腊语，而非梵语。」

「好哇！」梅利克嚷道，「我们总算把他引到梵语上来了！来吧，奥克森登，我的哥们儿，把《益言集》（Hitopadesa）、《云使》（Megha Dhuta）、《梨俱吠陀》（Rig Veda）都抖搂出来。引《贝奥武甫》（Beowulf），引凯德蒙（Caedmon）。再给我们来一点芝诺（Zeno），末了用近代波斯的《拉拉·鲁克》（Lalla Rookh）收梢。」

「所以我的结论是，」奥克森登平静地说，只当没听见梅利克的话，「科塞金人是一个闪米特民族。他们的肤色与须髯表明他们与高加索人种同源；他们的语言则毫无疑义地证明，他们属于该人种的闪米特分支。一个土生土长的民族，绝不可能有这样的语言。」

===Original===
“But how,” cried the doctor﻿—“how in the name of wonder did they get to the South Pole?”

“Easily enough,” interrupted Melick﻿—“Shem landed there from Noah’s ark, and left some of his children to colonize the country. That’s as plain as a pikestaff. I think, on the whole, that this idea is better than the other one about the Ten Tribes. At any rate they are both mine, and I warn all present to keep their hands off them, for on my return I intend to take out a copyright.”

===Chinese===
「可是，」医生嚷道——「他们究竟、究竟是怎么到南极去的呢？真是匪夷所思！」

「容易得很，」梅利克插嘴道——「闪（Shem）当年从挪亚方舟上在那儿登的陆，留下几个孩子殖民建国。这不是明摆着的事么。总的说来，我觉得这个主意比十支派那个还要高明。无论如何，两个都是我的发明，我警告诸位谁也不许染指，因为一回国，我就要去申领版权。」

===Original===
“There’s another thing,” continued Oxenden, “which is of immense importance, and that is their habit of cave-dwelling. I am inclined to think that they resorted to cave-dwelling at first from some hereditary instinct or other, and that their eyes and their whole morals have become affected by this mode of life. Now, as to ornamented caverns, we have many examples﻿—caverns adorned with a splendor fully equal to anything among the Kosekin. There are in India the great Behar caves, the splendid Karli temple with its magnificent sculptures and imposing architecture, and the cavern-temples of Elephanta; there are the subterranean works in Egypt, the temple of Dendera in particular; in Petra we have the case of an entire city excavated from the rocky mountains; yet, after all, these do not bear upon the point in question, for they are isolated cases; and even Petra, though it contained a city, did not contain a nation. But there is a case, and one which is well known, that bears directly upon this question, and gives us the connecting link between the Kosekin and their Semitic brethren in the northern hemisphere.”

“What is that?” asked the doctor.

“The Troglodytes,” said Oxenden, with impressive solemnity.

“Well, and what do you make out of the Troglodytes?”

===Chinese===
「还有一件事，」奥克森登接着说，「关系重大，那便是他们穴居的积习。我倾向于认为，他们起初是出于某种遗传的天性才栖身洞穴，而他们的眼睛和他们的整个风教心性，都是被这种生活方式浸染的。说到装点华美的洞窟，例证甚多——其富丽足可与科塞金人的任何洞府相埒。印度有宏大的比哈尔石窟，有雕刻壮丽、建筑巍峨的卡尔利寺窟，还有埃勒凡塔的洞窟神庙；埃及有地下宫室，尤以丹德拉神庙为最；在佩特拉，更有一整座从岩山中凿出的城。然而归根结底，这些都与此处讨论的问题无涉，因为它们都是孤例；佩特拉虽容一城，却未容一族。但有一个尽人皆知的先例，恰好切中这个问题，为我们搭起了科塞金人与他们北半球的闪米特同胞之间的联系之环。」

「那是什么？」医生问。

「特罗格洛迪特人（Troglodytes，穴居人）。」奥克森登以一种动人的庄重说道。

「那么，你从特罗格洛迪特人身上考究出什么来？」

===Original===
“I will explain,” said Oxenden. “The name Troglodytes is given to various tribes of men, but those best known and celebrated under this name once inhabited the shores of the Red Sea, both on the Arabian and the Egyptian side. They belonged to the Arabian race, and were consequently a Semitic people. Mark that, for it is a point of the utmost importance. Now, these Troglodytes all lived in caverns, which were formed partly by art and partly by nature, although art must have had most to do with the construction of such vast subterranean works. They lived in great communities in caverns, and they had long tunnels passing from one community to another. Here also they kept their cattle. Some of these people have survived even to our own age; for Bruce, the Abyssinian traveller, saw them in Nubia.

“The earliest writer who mentions the Troglodytes was Agatharcides, of Cnidos. According to him they were chiefly herdsmen. Their food was the flesh of cattle, and their drink a mixture of milk and blood. They dressed in the skins of cattle; they tattooed their bodies. They were very swift of foot, and were able to run down wild beasts in the hunt. They were also greatly given to robbery, and caravans passing to and fro had to guard against them.

“One feature in their character has to my mind a strange significance, and that is their feelings with regard to death. It was not the Kosekin love of death, yet it was something which must certainly be considered as approximating to it. For Agatharcides says that in their burials they were accustomed to fasten the corpse to a stake, and then gathering round, to pelt it with stones amid shouts of laughter and wild merriment. They also used to strangle the old and infirm, so as to deliver them from the evils of life. These Troglodytes, then, were a nation of cave-dwellers, loving the dark﻿—not exactly loving death, yet at any rate regarding it with merriment and pleasure; and so I cannot help seeing a connection between them and the Kosekin.”

===Chinese===
「我这就解释。」奥克森登说。「特罗格洛迪特人之名，加于形形色色的部落；但以此名见知于世、最著称的，昔年栖居于红海之滨，阿拉伯一侧与埃及一侧皆有。他们属于阿拉伯种族，因而是闪米特民族。记住这一点，这是至关重要的关节。这些特罗格洛迪特人全都居于洞窟之中；洞窟一部分出于天然，一部分出于人力，不过营造如此浩大的地下工程，人工想必居多。他们成大群落居于洞窟，还开有长长的隧道，从此一社群通往彼一社群；牲畜也养在里头。这族人有的竟一直存活到我们的时代；阿比西尼亚旅行家布鲁斯就在努比亚见过他们。」

「提及特罗格洛迪特人的最早作家，是克尼多斯的阿加塔尔基德斯（Agatharcides）。据他说，他们主要是牧人。他们以牛肉为食，以乳血相混为饮。他们身披牛皮，身上刺着黥文。他们健步如飞，围猎时能生生追及野兽。他们也颇惯于劫掠，往来商队都须提防他们。」

「他们的品性中有一端，在我看来意味深长，那便是他们对死亡的情感。那还不是科塞金人的尚死之癖，却断然可视为与之相邻近的东西。因为据阿加塔尔基德斯说，他们下葬时习惯把尸体缚在木桩上，然后围拢来，在阵阵哄笑与狂野的欢闹中朝尸身投掷石块。他们也惯于勒死年老衰迈的人，好把他们从人生的诸般苦难中解脱出来。这么看来，这些特罗格洛迪特人正是一个穴居的民族，爱恋黑暗——还不算酷爱死亡，至少也是以欢娱快意视之；因此我不由得看出他们同科塞金人之间的干系。」

===Original===
“Yes,” said the doctor, “but how did they get to the South Pole?”

“That,” said Oxenden, “is a question which I do not feel bound to answer.”

“Oh, it is easy enough to answer that,” said Melick. “They, of course, dug through the Earth.”

Oxenden gave a groan.

“I think I’ll turn in for the night,” said he, rising. Upon this the others rose also and followed his example.

===Chinese===
「是啊，」医生说，「可他们到底是怎么到南极去的呢？」

「那个嘛，」奥克森登说，「就不在我义务作答的范围之内了。」

「哦，这个好答得很。」梅利克说。「他们嘛，当然是从地球那头一路挖过来的。」

奥克森登发出一声呻吟。

「我看我该去睡了。」他说着站起身来。于是众人也都起身，学他的样各自归寝。

===Original===
On the following morning the calm still continued. None of the party rose until very late, and then over the breakfast-table they discussed the manuscript once more, each from his own point of view, Melick still asserting a contemptuous scepticism﻿—Oxenden and the doctor giving reasons for their faith, and Featherstone listening without saying much on either side.

At length it was proposed to resume the reading of the manuscript, which task would now devolve upon Oxenden. They adjourned to the deck, where all disposed themselves in easy attitudes to listen to the continuation of More’s narrative.

===Chinese===
次日清晨，风平浪静依旧。一行人直睡到日上三竿才起身，随后在早餐桌上又把那部手稿议论了一番，各人各说各的道理：梅利克依旧摆出一副鄙夷的怀疑论——奥克森登和医生各陈信其所信的缘由，费瑟斯通只管听着，两面都不多置一词。

末了，有人提议继续诵读手稿，这桩差事如今轮到奥克森登来承当。众人移步甲板，各自拣舒服的姿势坐定，听莫尔的叙述续往下讲。

## XVIII A Voyage Over the Pole / 十八、越极之航

===Original===
The discovery of our love had brought a crisis in our fate for me and Almah. The Kohen hailed it with joy, for now was the time when he would be able to present us to the Kohen Gadol. Our doom was certain and inevitable. We were to be taken to the *amir*; we were to be kept until the end of the dark season, and then we were both to be publicly sacrificed. After this our bodies were to be set apart for the hideous rites of the Mista Kosek. Such was the fate that lay before us.

The Kohen was now anxious to take us to the *amir*. I might possibly have persuaded him to postpone our departure, but I saw no use in that. It seemed better to go, for it was possible that amid new scenes and among new people there might be hope. This, too, seemed probable to Almah, who was quite anxious to go. The Kohen pressed forward the preparations, and at length a galley was ready for us.

This galley was about three hundred feet in length and fifty in width, but not more than six feet in depth. It was like a long raft. The rowers, two hundred in number, sat on a level with the water, one hundred on each side. The oars were small, being not more than twelve feet in length, but made of very light, tough material, with very broad blades. The galley was steered with broad-bladed paddles at both ends. There was no mast or sail. Astern was a light poop, surrounded by a pavilion, and forward there was another. At the bow there was a projecting platform, used chiefly in fighting the *thannin*, or sea-monsters, and also in war. There were no masts or flags or gay streamers; no brilliant colors; all was intensely black, and the ornaments were of the same hue.

We were now treated with greater reverence than ever, for we were looked upon as the recipients of the highest honor that could fall to any of the Kosekin﻿—namely, the envied dignity of a public death. As we embarked the whole city lined the public ways, and watched us from the quays, from boats, and from other galleys. Songs were sung by a chosen choir of paupers, and to the sound of this plaintive strain we moved out to sea.

===Chinese===
我和阿尔玛恋情败露，我们二人的命运也随之到了危急关头。科恩闻讯大喜，因为如今他终于可以把我们呈献给大祭司了。我们的劫数已定，在所难逃。我们将被带往阿米尔，被看押到黑暗季终了，然后双双当众献祭。此后我们的尸身还要被留出来，充米斯塔·科塞克那些可怖祭仪之用。摆在我们面前的，便是这样一条命途。

如今科恩一心要把我们送去阿米尔。我本或许能劝他把行期往后推一推，只是我看这全无用处。动身似是上策，因为换一番新的境地、新的人群，或许还有一线指望。这话在阿尔玛听来也颇近情理，她一心想要动身。科恩催促着备办行装，不多时便有一艘桨帆船为我们整治停当。

这艘桨帆船长可三百英尺，宽约五十英尺，船深却不过六英尺上下，活脱是一张加长的木筏。桨手凡二百名，座处与水面相齐，每侧各一百人。桨不大，长不过十二英尺，木料却极轻极韧，桨叶异常宽绰。船以首尾两处的宽叶大桨操舵。船上不设桅杆，也不张帆。船尾有一座轻巧的尾楼，围着顶篷；船首也有同样一座。船头前伸出一方平台，主要用于同海怪「坦宁」（thannin）搏杀，也用于战阵。全船无桅樯、无旗号、无彩带，无一分鲜明颜色，通体黑得深沉，连纹饰装点也是同一色。

我们如今所受的礼敬更胜从前，因为人们把我们看作科塞金人所能领受的最高荣誉的得主——也就是人人艳羡的公开处死的尊荣。登船之时，全城夹道，人们从码头、从舟艇、从别的桨帆船上争睹我们。一班精选的赤贫者唱诗班唱起歌来，我们便和着这哀婉的调门驶出了海。

===Original===
“This will be a great journey for me,” said the Kohen, as we left the port. “I hope to be made a pauper at least, and perhaps gain the honor of a public death. I have known people who have gained death for less. There was an Athon last year who attacked a *pehmet* with forty men and one hundred and twenty rowers. All were killed or drowned except himself. In reward for this he gained the *mudecheb*, or death recompense. In addition to this he was set apart for the Mista Kosek.”

“Then, with you, when a man procures the death of others he is honored?”

“Why, yes; how could it be otherwise?” said the Kohen. “Is it not the same with you? Have you not told me incredible things about your people, among which there were a few that seemed natural and intelligible? Among these was your system of honoring above all men those who procure the death of the largest number. You, with your pretended fear of death, wish to meet it in battle as eagerly as we do, and your most renowned men are those who have sent most to death.”

To this strange remark I had no answer to make.

===Chinese===
「这于我将是一次了不起的航行，」离港时科恩说道。「我指望至少能受封为赤贫者，说不定还能博得公开处死的荣典。我见过为着更小的缘由便挣得死亡的人。去年有一位阿通，率四十名兵卒、一百二十名桨手去攻打佩赫梅特（pehmet）。除了他本人，其余非死即溺，全军尽没。论功行赏，他得了穆德切布（mudecheb），也就是死亡酬赏；除此之外，他更被留备米斯塔·科塞克之用。」

「那么在你们这里，一个人替旁人谋得死亡，反倒受尊崇么？」

「可不是么？不然还能怎样？」科恩道，「你们那里难道两样？你可曾对我讲过你国中种种令人难以置信之事，其中倒有几件显得入情入理、可以理喻？这中间便有你们那套章法——凡替最多的人谋得死亡的，便尊于天下任何人。你们明明假作怕死，却同我们一样急切地巴望在兵戎之间与死亡相会；而你们最负盛名的人物，正是把最多的人送上死路的人。」

对这一番奇谈，我竟无从置答。

===Original===
The air out at sea now grew chillier. The Kohen noticed it also, and offered me his cloak, which I refused. He seemed surprised, and smiled.

“You are growing like one of us,” said he. “You will soon learn that the greatest happiness in life is to do good to others and sacrifice yourself. You already show this in part. When you are with Almah you act like one of the Kosekin. You watch her to see and anticipate her slightest wish; you are eager to give her everything. She, on the other hand, is equally eager to give up all to you. Each one of you is willing to lay down life for the other. You would gladly rush upon death to save her from harm, much as you pretend to fear death; and so I see that with Almah you will soon learn how sweet a thing death may be.”

“To live without her,” said I, “would be so bitter that death with her would indeed be sweet. If I could save her life by laying down my own, death would be sweeter still; and not one of you Kosekin would meet it so gladly.”

The Kosekin smiled joyously.

===Chinese===
外海的空气渐渐冷了。科恩也察觉了，便要将斗篷让给我，我辞谢不受。他似乎颇为诧异，莞尔一笑。

「你越来越像我们中人了，」他说。「你不久便会懂得，人生至乐，莫过于为他人造福、舍身忘己。这一点你已略见端倪。你同阿尔玛在一处时，行事便同一个科塞金人无异。你凝神看着她，她一丝一毫的心意你都要先着想到；你恨不得把一切都给她。她呢，也同样恨不得把一切都让给你。你们两个，个个都甘愿为对方舍弃性命。任凭你口口声声怕死，为使她免遭伤害，你会欢欢喜喜地迎着死亡扑上去；由此我看出，有阿尔玛在侧，你不久便会懂得死亡是何等甘美之事。」

「失了她而独活，」我说，「苦必彻骨；同她一处死，才真有几分甜。倘若舍了我这条命能换她一命，那死便更甜了；你们科塞金人当中，没有一个能像我这般欢然赴死。」

那位科塞金人喜逐颜开。

===Original===
“Oh, almighty and wondrous power of Love!” he exclaimed, “how thou hast transformed this foreigner! Oh, Atam-or! you will soon be one of us altogether. For see, how is it now? You pretend to love riches and life, and yet you are ready to give up everything for Almah.”

“Gladly, gladly!” I exclaimed.

“Yes,” he said, “all that you have﻿—you would gladly lavish on her, and would rejoice to make yourself a pauper for her sweet sake. You also would rejoice equally to give up life for her. Is it not so?”

“It is,” said I.

“Then I see by this that Almah has awakened within you your true human nature. Thus far it has lain dormant; it has been concealed under a thousand false and unnatural habits, arising from your strange native customs. You have been brought up under some frightful system, where nature is violated. Here among us your true humanity is unfolded, and with Almah you are like the Kosekin. Soon you will learn new lessons, and will find out that there is a new and a final self-abnegation in perfect love; and your love will never rest till you have separated yourself from Almah, so that love can have its perfect work.”

===Chinese===
「哦，爱情，全能而神奇的力量！」他欢呼道，「你把这般外乡人点化得何等彻底！哦，阿塔莫尔啊！你不久便要浑然是我们中人了。且看，如今是何光景？你口口声声恋着财富与生命，却甘愿为阿尔玛舍弃一切。」

「甘愿，甘愿！」我喊道。

「正是，」他说，「你所有的全部——你都乐于尽数倾注到她身上，甘心为她那可爱人的缘故沦为赤贫者，还满心欢喜。为了她，你同样乐于舍却性命。可不是这样么？」

「正是这样。」我说。

「如此说来，阿尔玛已把你天性中真正的人性唤醒了。它至今沉睡未醒，一直被千百种虚伪悖理的积习掩埋着——那些积习，皆从你故国古怪的风俗中生出。你是在一套戕贼天性的可怕制度下调教出来的。在我们这里，你真正的人性才得舒展；同阿尔玛在一处，你便同科塞金人一般无二。不久你还要领受新的功课，便会明白：至美的爱情里另有一重全新的、最后的自我舍弃；不把你同阿尔玛两下分开、好教爱情成就它圆满的功行，你的爱情便一日不得安歇。」

===Original===
The sea now opened wide before us, rising up high as if halfway to the zenith, giving the impression of a vast ascent to endless distances. Around the shores spread themselves, with the shadowy outlines of the mountains; above was the sky, all clear, with faint aurora-flashes and gleaming stars. Hand-in-hand with Almah I stood and pointed out the constellations as we marked them while she told me of the different divisions known among the Kosekin as well as her own people. There, high in the zenith, was the southern polar-star, not exactly at the pole, nor yet of very great brightness, but still sufficiently noticeable.

Looking back, we saw, low down, parts of the Phoenix and the Crane; higher up, the Toucan, Hydrus, and Pavo. On our right, low down, was the beautiful Altar; higher up, the Triangle; while on the left were the Swordfish and the Flying-fish. Turning to look forward, we beheld a more splendid display. Then, over the bow of the vessel, between the Centaur, which lay low, and Musca Indica, which rose high, there blazed the bright stars of the Southern Cross﻿—a constellation, if not the brightest, at least the most conspicuous and attractive in all the heavens. All around there burned other stars, separated widely. Then, over the stern, gleamed the splendid lustre of Achernar, on the left the brilliant glow of Alpha Robur and Canopus, and low down before us the bright light of Argo. It was a scene full of splendor and fascination. After a time a change came over the sky: the aurora-flashes, at first faint, gradually increased in brilliancy till the stars grew dim, and all the sky, wherever the eye might turn from the horizon to the zenith, seemed filled with lustrous flames of every conceivable hue. Colossal beams radiated from the pole toward the horizon till the central light was dissipated, and there remained encircling us an infinite colonnade of flaming pillars that towered to the stars. These were all in motion, running upon one another, incessantly shifting and changing; new scenes forever succeeded to old; pillars were transformed to pyramids, pyramids to fiery bars; these in their turn were transformed to other shapes, and all the while one tint of innumerable hues overspread the entire circle of the sky.

===Chinese===
这时海面在我们眼前豁然展开，向上高高隆起，仿佛直抵半天，给人以一种浩浩登临、绵延无尽之感。四围海岸铺陈开去，衬出群山朦胧的轮廓；头顶天空澄澈，极光幽幽闪烁，群星灼灼生辉。我携了阿尔玛的手伫立着，指点星辰，逐一辨认；她便给我讲科塞金人和她本族各自通行的星座分野。高悬天顶的，是那颗南极星，虽不恰在极点之上，也谈不上十分明亮，却仍旧足以辨认。

回望来路，低处见凤凰座与天鹤座的一部；再高些，是杜鹃座、水蛇座和孔雀座。右手边低处，是美丽的祭坛座；高处是三角座；左手边则是剑鱼座和飞鱼座。转身向前望去，眼前更是壮观。船头之外，在低垂的半人马座与高悬的苍蝇座（Musca Indica）之间，南十字座（Southern Cross）的亮星灼灼燃烧——这星座纵非全天最亮，至少也是全天最显赫、最动人心目的一个。四周还有别的星辰灼然在燃，彼此相隔迢遥。船尾上方，闪动着水委一（Achernar）璀璨的光华；左边是橡树座α星（Alpha Robur）与老人星（Canopus）灼灼的辉光；前方低处，是南船座（Argo）明亮的星光。这一派景象，壮丽夺目，令人心醉。过了一晌，天空换了一副面目：起初幽微的极光渐渐明亮，直到星光为之黯淡；放眼望去，自天际直至天顶，整个天空仿佛燃满了种种意想所不及的色相的明焰。硕大无朋的光柱自极点向天际四射，直到中央光华散尽，只剩下一圈无穷无尽的火柱环列我们四周，巍然直上星辰。这些光柱无一刻凝定，彼此奔逐，须臾不停地移形换状；新景层出不穷，旧景转瞬即逝；光柱化作金字塔，金字塔化作烈焰长杠；这些又辗转化作别样形状；而与此同时，万千色相流转漫覆，罩满了周天一圈的天空。

===Original===
Our voyage occupied several *joms*; but our progress was continuous, for different sets of rowers relieved one another at regular intervals. On the second *jom* a storm broke out. The sky had been gathering clouds during sleeping-time, and when we awoke we found the sea all lashed to fury, while all around the darkness was intense. The storm grew steadily worse; the lightning flashed, the thunder pealed, and at length the sea was so heavy that rowing was impossible. Upon this the oars were all taken in, and the galley lay tossing upon the furious sea, amid waves that continually beat upon her.

And now a scene ensued that filled me with amazement, and took away all my thoughts from the storm. It seemed impossible that so frail a bark could stand the fury of the waves. Destruction was inevitable, and I was expecting to see the usual signs of grief and despair﻿—wondering, too, how these rowers would preserve their subordination. But I had forgotten in my excitement the strange nature of the Kosekin. Instead of terror there was joy, instead of wild despair there was peace and serene delight.

The lightning-flashes revealed a wonderful scene. There were all the rowers, each one upon his seat, and from them all there came forth a chant which was full of triumph, like a song of public welcome to some great national hero, or a song of joy over victory. The officers embraced one another and exchanged words of delight. The Kohen, after embracing all the others, turned to me, and, forgetting my foreign ways, exclaimed, in a tone of enthusiastic delight,

===Chinese===
我们的航程行了几个约姆；船却始终行进不辍，因为各班桨手按时轮替，接续不休。第二个约姆上，起了风暴。睡眠时分天上已在聚云，待我们醒来，只见海面怒涛翻卷，四外一片深黑。风暴愈演愈烈；电闪，雷鸣，到后来浪涛滔天，划桨已无可能。于是桨尽数收起，桨帆船任凭怒海颠簸摆布，波涛一阵阵扑打船身。

这时眼前出现了一幕，使我惊诧不已，连风暴也忘在了脑后。这样单薄的一条船，看来万万经不起怒涛的肆虐。覆灭已在所难免，我正等着看惯常那种悲恸绝望的景象——心里还盘算着，这些桨手届时又将如何维持他们的等第统属。可我在惶急之中，竟把科塞金人的古怪天性忘了个干净。这里没有恐怖，只有欢喜；没有狂乱的绝望，只有安宁与恬静的喜悦。

电光起处，照见一幕奇景。众桨手个个端坐桨位，齐声迸出一支凯歌，满含得意，仿佛举国欢迎某位民族大英雄的颂曲，或是庆祝大捷的欢歌。众将官相互拥抱，互道欣忭。科恩同众人都拥抱过了，便转向我，一时间忘了我是外乡人，以一片狂喜的声调叫道：

===Original===
“We are destroyed! Death is near! Rejoice!”

Accustomed as I was to the perils of the sea, I had learned to face death without flinching. Almah, too, was calm, for to her this death seemed preferable to that darker fate which awaited us; but the words of the Kohen jarred upon my feelings.

“Do you not intend to do anything to save the ship?” I asked.

He laughed joyously.

===Chinese===
「我们完啦！死亡临头！欢喜吧！」

我虽久历海上凶险，早已学会临死不惧；阿尔玛也很镇定，因为在她看来，这样的死总比等着我们的那桩更黑暗的命运强些；然而科恩这话，听得我心里发涩。

「你们不打算想点什么法子救这条船么？」我问。

他欢畅地笑了。

===Original===
“There’s no occasion,” said he. “When the oars are taken in we always begin to rejoice. And why not? Death is near﻿—it is almost certain. Why should we do anything to distract our minds and mar our joy? For oh, dear friend, the glorious time has come when we can give up life﻿—life, with all its toils, its burdens, its endless bitternesses, its perpetual evils. Now we shall have no more suffering from vexatious and oppressive riches, from troublesome honors, from a surplus of food, from luxuries and delicacies, and all the ills of life.”

“But what is the use of being born at all?” I asked, in a wonder that never ceased to rise at every fresh display of Kosekin feeling.

“The use?” said the Kohen. “Why, if we were not born, how could we know the bliss of dying, or enjoy the sweetness of death? Death is the end of being﻿—the one sweet hope and crown and glory of life, the one desire and hope of every living man. The blessing is denied to none. Rejoice with me, oh Atam-or! you will soon know its blessedness as well as I.”

===Chinese===
「用不着，」他说。「每逢收桨，我们总要开始欢庆。为什么不呢？死亡已近——几乎是笃定的了。何苦再做什么来分心乱意、败我们的高兴？哦，亲爱的朋友，荣耀的时辰到了，我们可以撇下生命了——生命，连同它一切的辛劳、一切的重负、无尽的苦涩、绵延的灾殃。从此我们再不必受恼人财富的重压之苦、烦人荣衔之累之苦、饮食过剩之苦、珍馐美馔之苦，再不必受人生种种祸殃了。」

「那么人究竟何苦要生下来呢？」我问道。科塞金人的心性每有新的流露，我心头的惊异便随之再涨一层，此刻正是如此。

「何苦？」科恩道。「唉，若不生下来，我们又怎能领受死的至福、尝到死亡的甘美？死亡是生涯的归宿——是人生唯一甜美的指望、唯一的冠冕与荣光，是每一个活人唯一的欲求与盼望。这份福分，无人不得沾溉。同我一起欢乐吧，哦，阿塔莫尔！它的福祉如何，你不久也要同我一样了然。」

===Original===
He turned away. I held Almah in my arms, and we watched the storm by the lightning-flashes and waited for the end. But the end came not. The galley was light, broad, and buoyant as a lifeboat; at the same time it was so strongly constructed that there was scarcely any twist or contortion in the sinewy fabric. So we floated buoyantly and safely upon the summit of vast waves, and a storm that would have destroyed a ship of the European fashion scarcely injured this in the slightest degree. It was an indestructible as a raft and as buoyant as a bubble; so we rode out the gale, and the death which the Kosekin invoked did not come at all.

The storm was but short-lived; the clouds dispersed, and soon went scudding over the sky; the sea went down. The rowers had to take their oars once more, and the reaction that followed upon their recent rejoicing was visible in universal gloom and dejection. As the clouds dispersed the aurora lights came out more splendid than ever, and showed nothing but melancholy faces. The rowers pulled with no life or animation; the officers stood about sighing and lamenting; Almah and I were the only ones that rejoiced over this escape from death.

*Joms* passed. We saw other sights; we met with galleys and saw many ships about the sea. Some were moved by sails only; these were merchant ships, but they had only square sails, and could not sail in any other way than before the wind. Once or twice I caught glimpses of vast shadowy objects in the air. I was startled and terrified; for, great as were the wonders of this strange region, I had not yet suspected that the air itself might have denizens as tremendous as the land or the sea. Yet so it was, and afterward during the voyage I saw them often. One in particular was so near that I observed it with ease. It came flying along in the same course with us, at a height of about fifty feet from the water. It was a frightful monster, with a long body and vast wings like those a bat. Its progress was swift, and it soon passed out of sight. To Almah the monster created no surprise; she was familiar with them, and told me that they were very abundant here, but that they never were known to attack ships. She informed me that they were capable of being tamed if caught when young, though in her country they were never made use of. The name given by the Kosekin to these monsters is *athaleb*.

At length we drew near to our destination. We reached a large harbor at the end of a vast bay: here the mountains extended around, and before us there arose terrace after terrace of twinkling lights running away to immense distances. It looked like a city of a million inhabitants, though it may have contained far less than that. By the brilliant aurora light I could see that it was in general shape and form precisely like the city that we had left, though far larger and more populous. The harbor was full of ships and boats of all sorts, some lying at the stone quays, others leaving port, others entering. Galleys passed and repassed, and merchant ships with their clumsy sails, and small fishing-boats. From afar arose the deep hum of a vast multitude and the low roar that always ascends from a popular city.

===Chinese===
他转身去了。我把阿尔玛搂在怀中，借着电光看风暴肆虐，静候末日。可末日竟没有来。这条桨帆船轻巧、宽绰，像救生艇一般浮力十足；同时又造得异常坚固，坚韧的船骨几乎不见一丝扭绞变形。于是我们稳稳地、轻飘飘地浮在巨浪的浪尖之上；一场足以毁掉欧式海船的风暴，竟几乎没让它受一点损伤。它像木筏一样砸不烂，像水泡一样沉不了；我们就这样挨过了大风，而科塞金人千祈万祷的那个死，终究没有来。

风暴不过昙花一现；云散了，不久便掠着天际疾驰而去；海也平息下来。桨手们只得重新操桨，方才那场欢庆过后接踵而来的心绪逆转，化作了满船的愁惨颓丧。云开之处，极光放出比先前更盛的光华，照出来的却尽是悒悒的面孔。桨手们划桨有气无力；将官们四下散立，唉声叹气；为这次死里逃生而欢喜的，只有阿尔玛和我两个。

约姆一个接一个过去。我们又见了些别的景致；一路遇见别的桨帆船，望见海上许多船只。有些单凭帆力行驶；那是商船，却只张横帆，唯有顺风一途可行。有一两回，我瞥见空中有庞大的黑影。我又惊又骇；因为这奇乡异境纵然奇观迭出，我却还不曾疑心连天空本身也会有如陆地与海洋一般骇人的居民。然而竟真有其事，此后一路上我时常望见它们。有一只飞得极近，我可以从容细看。它顺着我们的航路飞来，离水面约莫五十英尺。好一个骇人的怪物：长长的身子，张开蝙蝠般的一双巨翼。它行进如飞，不多时便没了踪影。这怪物在阿尔玛眼里毫不新奇；她见惯了，还告诉我说此间这种东西极多，只是从未听说它们袭击船只。她又告诉我，若在幼雏时捕获，它们驯得服帖，只是在她本国从无人驱用。科塞金人给这些怪物起的名字，叫作阿塔莱布（athaleb）。

我们终于驶近了目的地。在一大片海湾的尽头，我们进了一座大港：群山由此环抱开去，眼前层层梯台灯火点点，次第而起，迤逦延展到极远处。看去俨然一座百万之众的大城，虽然实际人口或许远逊于此。借着灿烂的极光，我看得出它的大体形制同我们离开的那座城如出一辙，只是远为宏大，人烟也远为稠密。港中泊满形形色色的船艇，有的靠在石头码头边，有的正在出港，有的正在进港。桨帆船穿梭往来，还有张着笨重帆篷的商船、小小的渔船。远处升起万千人众低沉的嗡嗡声，那是人烟辐辏的大城永远不断的一声低吼。

===Original===
The galley hauled alongside her wharf, and we found ourselves at length in the mighty *amir* of the Kosekin. The Kohen alone landed; the rest remained on board, and Almah and I with them.

Other galleys were here. On the wharf workmen were moving about. Just beyond were caverns that looked like warehouses. Above these was a terraced street, where a vast multitude moved to and fro﻿—a living tide as crowded and as busy as that in Cheapside.

After what seemed a long time the Kohen returned. This time he came with a number of people, all of whom were in cars drawn by *opkuks*. Half were men and half women. These came aboard, and it seemed as though we were to be separated; for the women took Almah, while the men took me. Upon this I entreated the Kohen not to separate us. I informed him that we were both of a different race from his, that we did not understand their ways; we should be miserable if separated.

===Chinese===
桨帆船靠上自家码头，我们终于置身于科塞金人巍巍的阿米尔。只有科恩一人登岸；其余人等都留在船上，阿尔玛和我也在其中。

这里还泊着别的桨帆船。码头上工役们来来往往。再往里去是一些窟穴，看去像货仓。窟穴之上是一条梯台街，浩浩人潮往来流动——那人潮之拥挤、之忙碌，不减于奇普赛德（Cheapside）街头。

仿佛过了许久，科恩回来了。这一回他带来了好些人，全都乘着奥普库克挽拉的车子，男女各半。这些人上了船，看来我们就要被分开了；因为女人们领走阿尔玛，男人们则来看住我。见此情形，我恳求科恩不要拆散我们。我告诉他，我们俩与他并不同族，不懂他们的规矩；一旦分开，我们只有愁苦。

===Original===
I spoke long and with all the entreaty possible to one with my limited acquaintance with the language. My words evidently impressed them: some of them even wept.

“You make us sad,” said the Kohen. “Willingly would we do everything that you bid, for we are your slaves; but the state law prevents. Still, in your case, the law will be modified; for you are in such honor here that you may be considered as beyond the laws. For the present, at least, we cannot separate you.”

These words brought much consolation. After this we landed, and Almah and I were still together.

===Chinese===
我说了许久，以我对此邦语言有限的掌握，把恳求的本事使到了十分。我的话显然打动了他们：有几个竟落了泪。

「你叫我们心里难受，」科恩说。「凡你所吩咐的，我们都乐意从命，因为我们本是你的奴隶；只是国法不容。不过就你们二位而论，法律自当从权；你们在此间尊荣如此之隆，尽可视作超乎法律之外。至少目前，我们不能拆散你们。」

这话带来了莫大的安慰。此后我们登了岸，阿尔玛和我依旧在一处。

## XIX The Wonders of the *Amir* / 十九、阿米尔的奇观

===Original===
We were drawn on cars up to the first terraced street, and here we found the vast multitude which we had seen from a distance. Crossing this street, we ascended and came to another precisely like it; then, still going on, we came to a third. Here there was an immense space, not overgrown with trees like the streets, but perfectly open. In the midst arose a lofty pyramid, and as I looked at it I could not refrain from shuddering; for it looked like the public altar, upon which in due time I should be compelled to make my appearance, and be offered up as a victim to the terrific superstitions of the Kosekin.

Crossing this great square, we came to a vast portal, which opened into a cavern with twinkling lights. The city itself extended above this, for we could see the terraced streets rising above our heads; but here our progress ended at the great cavern in the chief square, opposite the pyramid.
===Chinese===
我们坐着车，被拉上了第一层梯台街道，在这里遇上了先前远远望见的那大片人群。穿过这条街，往上再走，来到一条一模一样的街；继续前行，又到了第三条。这里有一片广阔无垠的场地，不像各条街道那样树木蔓生，而是全然开阔。场地中央耸起一座高峻的金字塔；我望着它，禁不住打起寒噤：因为它看上去正像那座公共祭坛——到了时候，我便要被逼着登上那上面去现身，作为祭牲，被献给科塞金人那骇人的迷信。

穿过这片大广场，我们来到一道巨大的门洞前，门内通向一个灯火闪烁的洞窟。城池本身由此向上延展，因为我们抬头看得见层层梯台街道在头顶上叠起；但我们的行程到此为止——停在主广场上与金字塔相对的这座巨窟里。
===Original===
On entering the cavern we traversed an antechamber, and then passing on we reached a vast dome, of dimensions so great that I could perceive no end in that gloom. The twinkling lights served only to disclose the darkness and to indicate the immensity of the cavern. In the midst there arose two enormous columns, which were lost in the gloom above.

It was only by passing through this that we learned its great extent. We at length came to the other end, and here we saw numerous passages leading away. The Kohen led us through one of these, and after passing through several other domes of smaller dimensions we at length reached an apartment where we stopped. This place was furnished with couches and hangings, and lighted with flaming lamps. The light was distressing to those who had accompanied us, and many of them left, while the few who remained had to cover their eyes. Here we found that all preparations had been made. The apartments were all illuminated, though our love of light never ceased to be a matter of amazement to the Kosekin, and a bounteous repast was spread for us. But the Kohen and the others found the light intolerable, and soon left us to ourselves.
===Chinese===
走进洞窟，先穿过一处前厅，继续前行，便来到一座穹顶巨窟，其规模之大，在那幽暗之中我竟望不见尽头。闪烁的灯火只是照出黑暗，显出窟穴的浩大而已。正中耸立着两根巨大的石柱，柱身隐没在上方的幽暗里。

唯有打这窟殿一路穿行过去，我们才领教它何等浩大。我们终于走到另一头，只见许多甬道从这里四散引开。科恩领我们走进其中一条，又穿过几座规模较小的穹顶窟殿，终于到了一处停歇的居室。这地方陈设着卧榻帷幔，点着熊熊的灯。灯光叫随行的人们苦不堪言，许多人走了，留下的几个也不得不掩住眼睛。在这里我们发现一切早已备妥：各间居室都灯火通明——我们之酷爱光明，在科塞金人看来始终是一桩奇事——还有一席丰盛的肴馔为我们摆开。但科恩与其余人受不了这光，不多时便撇下我们走了。
===Original===
After the repast some women appeared to take Almah to her chamber, and, with the usual kindness of the Kosekin, they assured her that she would not be expected to obey the law of separation, but that she was to remain here, where she would be always within reach of me.

After her departure there came to visit me the lowest man in all the land of the Kosekin, though, according to our view, he would be esteemed the highest. This was the *Kohen Gadol*. His history had already been told me. I had learned that through lack of Kosekin virtue he had gradually sunk to this position, and now was compelled to hold in his hands more wealth, power, and display than any other man in the nation.
===Chinese===
宴罢，来了几名女子，领阿尔玛去她的寝室；她们还怀着科塞金人惯常的好意，向她保证说，并不指望她遵行那分离仪式的法则，她只管住在这里，随时都在我的近旁。

她走之后，来探访我的是科塞金全国至低贱的人——虽然照我们的看法，他该被尊为至高。这便是大祭司科恩·加多尔（Kohen Gadol）。他的来历早已有人讲给我听过：我得知他因缺乏科塞金人的德行，渐渐沦落到这个地步，如今被迫把比国中任何人都要多的财富、权势与煊赫握在手中。
===Original===
He was a man of singular appearance. The light was not so troublesome to him as to the others﻿—he merely kept his eyes shaded; but he regarded me with a keen look of inquiry that was suggestive of shrewdness and cunning. I confess it was with a feeling of relief that I made this discovery; for I longed to find someone among this singular people who was selfish, who feared death, who loved life, who loved riches, and had something in common with me. This I thought I perceived in the shrewd, cunning face of the Kohen Gadol, and I was glad; for I saw that while he could not possibly be more dangerous to me than those self-sacrificing, self-denying cannibals whom I had thus far known, he might prove of some assistance, and might help me to devise means of escape. If I could only find someone who was a coward, and selfish and avaricious﻿—if this Kohen Gadol could but be he﻿—how much brighter my life would be! And so there happened to me an incredible thing, that my highest wish was now to find in the Kohen Gadol cowardice, avarice, and selfishness.

The Kohen was accompanied by a young female, richly attired, who, I afterward learned, was his daughter. Her name was Layelah, and she filled the office of *Malca*, which signifies queen; and though honorable with us above all, is among the Kosekin the lowest in the land. Layelah was so beautiful that I looked at her in amazement. She was very tall for one of the Kosekin, which made her stature equal to that of an ordinary girl with us; her hair was rich, dark and luxuriant, gathered about her head in great masses and bound by a golden band. Her features were delicate and perfect in their outline; her expression was noble and commanding. Her eyes were utterly unlike those of the other Kosekin; the upper lids had a slight droop, but that was all, and that was the nearest approach to the national blink. Her first entrance into the room seemed to dazzle her, and she shaded her eyes for a few moments, but after that she looked at me fixedly, and seemed to suffer no more inconvenience than I did. The perfect liberty of women among the Kosekin made this visit from her quite as natural as that of her father; and though she said but little on this occasion, she was an attentive listener and close observer.
===Chinese===
他是个相貌奇特的人。光之于他，不像之于旁人那样难熬——他只是拿东西给眼睛遮了遮光；但他打量我时，目光锐利，带着刨根问底的探究，透出精明与狡黠。我承认，发现这一点时，我暗自松了口气；因为我一直渴望在这奇族里找到一个自私、怕死、恋生、爱财、与我有点相通之处的人。我想，我在大祭司那张精明狡黠的脸上看出的正是这个，不由得心中欢喜；因为我看出，他纵然不见得会比我迄今结识的那些舍身克己的食人者更危险，却说不定能助我一臂之力，帮我想出脱身的法子。倘若我能找到一个懦夫——又自私、又贪财——倘若这大祭司恰是此人，我的日子该会多添几分光明！于是我遇上了一桩难以置信的事：我如今最大的心愿，竟是在大祭司身上寻出怯懦、贪婪与自私来。

与科恩同来的，是一位衣饰华贵的年轻女子；我后来才知道，她是他的女儿。她名叫莱拉（Layelah），居玛尔卡（Malca）之职——此语意为女王；这职位在我们那里尊贵无以复加，在科塞金人中间却是全国至低。莱拉美得叫我惊异地注视不已。她在科塞金人中身量算极高的，即便如此，也只及我们那里寻常少女的个头；她一头美发，乌黑丰茂，堆拥在头颅四周，束着一道金带。她眉目纤秀，轮廓完美；神情高贵而有威仪。她的双眼与其余科塞金人的全然不同：上眼睑只是微微低垂，如此而已——这已是与国人通有的眨视最相近的模样了。她初进屋时似乎被光晃得睁不开眼，遮了一阵眼睛，过后便定定地望着我，看上去不比我更觉难受。科塞金的女子享有充分的自由，她的来访同她父亲的一样自然；这一回她虽话说得少，却是个入神的听者、细密的观察者。
===Original===
Their visit was long, for they were evidently full of curiosity. They had heard much about me and wished to see more. It was the first time that I had found among the Kosekin the slightest desire to know where I had come from. Hitherto all had been content with the knowledge that I was a foreigner. Now, however, I found in the Kohen Gadol and Layelah a curiosity that was most eager and intense. They questioned me about my country, about the great world beyond the mountains, about the way in which I had come here, about the manners and customs of my countrymen. They were eager to know about those great nations of which I spoke, who loved light and life; about men who loved themselves better than others; of that world where men feared death and loved life, and sought after riches and lived in the light.

The sleeping-time came and passed, and my visitors were still full of eager questionings. It was Layelah who at last thought of the lateness of the hour. At a word from her the Kohen Gadol rose, with many apologies, and prepared to go. But before he left he said:
===Chinese===
他们这一坐便坐了很久，因为两人显然满怀好奇。关于我，他们已听得不少，还想看得更多。这是我在科塞金人中间头一回发现哪怕一丝想知道我从何处来的意愿。在此之前，众人只消知道我是个外乡人便已满足。如今，我却在他们父女身上见到一种急切而炽烈的好奇。他们问我的故国，问群山之外的那个大千世界，问我来此的路径，问我国人的风土人情。他们急欲知道我所讲的那些热爱光明与生命的泱泱大国；知道那些爱自己甚于爱旁人的人；知道那个人人怕死恋生、追求财富、生活在光明里的世界。

睡眠时分来了又过去，我的访客们还在急切地追问不休。末了，还是莱拉想起时候不早。经她一句话，大祭司连连告罪，起身准备离去。临走之前，他说道：
===Original===
“When I was a child I was shipwrecked, and was taken up a ship which conveyed me to a nation beyond the sea. There I grew up to manhood. I learned their language and manners and customs, and when I returned home I found myself an alien here: I do not love darkness or death, I do not hate riches, and the result is that I am what I am. If I were like the rest of my countrymen, my lot would make me miserable; but as it is I prefer it to any other, and consider myself not the lowest but the greatest in the land. My daughter is like me, and instead of being ashamed of her station she is proud of it, and would not give it up even to become a pauper. I will see you again. I have much to say.”

With these words the Kohen Gadol retired, followed by Layelah, leaving me more hopeful than I had been for a long time.
===Chinese===
「我幼年时遭遇海难，被一艘船救起，载往海外的一个国度。我在那里长大成人，学会了他们的语言、礼数与习俗；待我回到家乡，却发觉自己在这里成了异乡之人：我不爱黑暗，也不爱死亡；我不恨财富；结果，我便成了如今这般光景。倘若我同其余的同胞一样，我的命运早叫我愁苦不堪了；可如今这样，我反倒觉得它胜过其他一切，认为自己在国中不是至低，而是至大。我女儿与我相像：她不以自己的地位为耻，反引以为傲，哪怕让她去做赤贫者，她也不肯把这地位换出去。我还会来见你。我有许多话要说。」

说完这话，大祭司便告退出去，莱拉跟在后面；他这一走，倒把我留在了许久以来未有的指望之中。
===Original===
For many *joms* following I received visits from the Kohen Gadol and from Layelah. Almah was with me until sleeping-time, and then these other visitors would come. In this, at least, they resembled the other Kosekin, that they never dreamed of interfering with Almah when she might wish to be with me. Their visits were always long, and we had much to say; but what I lost of sleep I always made up on the following *jom*. The Kohen Gadol, with his keen, shrewd face, interested me greatly; but Layelah, with her proud face and air of command, was a positive wonder.

I soon learned that the Kohen Gadol was what we term “a man of advanced views,” or perhaps a “Reformer,” or a “Philosophic Radical,” it matters not which; suffice it to say that his ideas and feelings differed from those of his nation, and if carried out would be equal to a revolution in politics and morals.
===Chinese===
此后许多约姆（jom），我接连受着大祭司和莱拉的来访。阿尔玛陪我到睡眠时分，随后这两位便来。至少在这一点上，他们与其余科塞金人如出一辙：凡是阿尔玛愿同我在一起的时辰，他们连想也不曾想去搅扰。他们的来访总是很久，我们有许多话可谈；睡眠上亏欠的，我总在下一个约姆补足。大祭司那张精明锐利的脸叫我大感兴趣；而莱拉——凭着那张高傲的脸与发号施令的气概——简直是一桩不折不扣的奇观。

我很快便弄明白了：大祭司正是我们所谓的「见解放达之士」，或者叫「改革家」，或者叫「哲学激进派（Philosophic Radical）」——名目无关紧要；只消这样说就够了：他的思想与感情皆与其国人相异，一旦见诸施行，便无异于政治与道德上的一场革命。
===Original===
The Kohen Gadol advocated selfishness as the true law of life, without which no state can prosper. There were a few of similar views, but they were all regarded with great contempt by the multitude, and had to suffer the utmost rigor of the law; for they were all endowed with vast wealth, compelled to live in the utmost splendor and luxury, to have enormous retinues, and to wield the chief power in politics and in religion.
